That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. When I look in the mirror, it just looks so real to me that it's hard to imagine it being any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a doctor or other clinician examine your nose, jaw, or skin and tell you that there is no actual deformity or abnormality?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't actually seen a doctor or any kind of specialist to examine my face or skin. I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too."

A series of safety trainings and promotional videos required him to be photographed and filmed closely, and younger crew members started posting candid shots of the team on social media, which he would fixate on for hours at home, zooming in on features he felt looked \"wrong.\" Over the next couple of years he started turning down foreman roles that involved meeting architects or clients, arriving late to the Bay Area Runners club because he was stuck in the locker room trying to \"fix\" how he looked, and avoiding post-run coffee with the group, which his friends and family noticed as a marked change. He finally agreed to see a psychiatrist after an HR manager raised concerns about his repeated refusal to appear in mandatory site photography and his missed shifts, and his sister, worried about how isolated and agitated he seemed whenever pictures of him came up, pushed him to get professional help.\n- Chief Complaint: “I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S067 (Preoccupation_with_Appearance): When I see those close-up photos from the job site or the running club, all I can focus on is how battered and crooked my face looks—like my nose is bent, my jaw is off, and my skin looks like old leather—and I’ll sit there for hours zooming in and thinking, ‘How can anyone stand to look at me like this?’\n- S068 (Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors): I lose huge chunks of time standing in the bathroom mirror or scrolling through every picture of me on my phone, tilting my head different ways, picking at my skin, shaving and re-shaving, changing shirts three or four times, and asking my sister or my kids over and over if I look “lopsided” before I finally give up and either show up late or just skip work or the run altogether.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve turned down foreman roles, called in sick or shown up late because I couldn’t face being photographed or seen up close, and I’ve pretty much stopped hanging out with my running group after races, so my paycheck and social life have both taken a real hit.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs. It’s pretty embarrassing to admit at my age, but it's gotten to the point where I just can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or at photos, do you find yourself focusing on one or more specific parts of your body that you believe look flawed or ugly, even if other people say they look normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's my face—I can't stop focusing on how bent my nose looks, my jaw being off, and my skin looking like old leather. I'll spend hours zooming in on photos of myself and constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided. It's incredibly stressful because to me, it just looks completely wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these appearance concerns could be exaggerated by your mind, or are you completely convinced that your nose, jaw, or skin really are abnormal or deformed?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced it’s real and not just in my head. To me, my nose looks undeniably bent, my jaw is totally off, and my skin looks like old leather. That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. When I look in the mirror, it just looks so real to me that it's hard to imagine it being any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a doctor or other clinician examine your nose, jaw, or skin and tell you that there is no actual deformity or abnormality?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't actually seen a doctor or any kind of specialist to examine my face or skin. I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe that other people are talking about your appearance, laughing at you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: I don't think people are necessarily laughing at me or lying to my face about it. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?"
  }

No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.

It's incredibly stressful because to me, it just looks completely wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these appearance concerns could be exaggerated by your mind, or are you completely convinced that your nose, jaw, or skin really are abnormal or deformed?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced it’s real and not just in my head. To me, my nose looks undeniably bent, my jaw is totally off, and my skin looks like old leather. That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. When I look in the mirror, it just looks so real to me that it's hard to imagine it being any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a doctor or other clinician examine your nose, jaw, or skin and tell you that there is no actual deformity or abnormality?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't actually seen a doctor or any kind of specialist to examine my face or skin. I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe that other people are talking about your appearance, laughing at you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: I don't think people are necessarily laughing at me or lying to my face about it. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely."

It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me."
  }

I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?"

I'll spend hours zooming in on photos of myself and constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided. It's incredibly stressful because to me, it just looks completely wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these appearance concerns could be exaggerated by your mind, or are you completely convinced that your nose, jaw, or skin really are abnormal or deformed?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced it’s real and not just in my head. To me, my nose looks undeniably bent, my jaw is totally off, and my skin looks like old leather. That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. When I look in the mirror, it just looks so real to me that it's hard to imagine it being any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a doctor or other clinician examine your nose, jaw, or skin and tell you that there is no actual deformity or abnormality?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't actually seen a doctor or any kind of specialist to examine my face or skin. I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe that other people are talking about your appearance, laughing at you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: I don't think people are necessarily laughing at me or lying to my face about it. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?"
  }
]

I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.

I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?\n\nPatient: To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.

I'll spend hours zooming in on photos of myself and constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided. It's incredibly stressful because to me, it just looks completely wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these appearance concerns could be exaggerated by your mind, or are you completely convinced that your nose, jaw, or skin really are abnormal or deformed?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced it’s real and not just in my head. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to consider that your appearance concerns might be exaggerated or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain all the time that your face is deformed?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It's hard to say because the flaws feel completely real and obvious to me, so I haven't really thought about whether it's exaggerated. When I look at myself, what I see just feels like the absolute truth.

That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?\n\nPatient: To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to consider that your appearance concerns might be exaggerated or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain all the time that your face is deformed?\n\nPatient: It's hard to say because the flaws feel completely real and obvious to me, so I haven't really thought about whether it's exaggerated. When I look at myself, what I see just feels like the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: When you are not looking in the mirror or at photos, do you still believe with complete certainty that your face is deformed, or can you acknowledge there is a chance you may be misperceiving it?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Even when I'm not looking, the worry is always there and it feels incredibly real and distressing to me. I honestly don't know how to answer whether it's a misperception or if I'm absolutely certain, but it just constantly weighs on me.

Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe that other people are talking about your appearance, laughing at you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: I don't think people are necessarily laughing at me or lying to my face about it. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?\n\nPatient: To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to consider that your appearance concerns might be exaggerated or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain all the time that your face is deformed?\n\nPatient: It's hard to say because the flaws feel completely real and obvious to me, so I haven't really thought about whether it's exaggerated. When I look at myself, what I see just feels like the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: When you are not looking in the mirror or at photos, do you still believe with complete certainty that your face is deformed, or can you acknowledge there is a chance you may be misperceiving it?\n\nPatient: Even when I'm not looking, the worry is always there and it feels incredibly real and distressing to me. I honestly don't know how to answer whether it's a misperception or if I'm absolutely certain, but it just constantly weighs on me.\n\nDoctor: When you are away from mirrors or photos, can you acknowledge that there is a chance your face may not actually be deformed, or are you completely certain it is deformed all the time?"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s really hard to say, and I’m not completely sure how to answer that. Even when I'm not looking in a mirror, I just can't shake the feeling that something is terribly wrong with my face. It always feels so real to me in the moment, so it's tough to know if there's a chance I'm just misperceiving it.

A series of safety trainings and promotional videos required him to be photographed and filmed closely, and younger crew members started posting candid shots of the team on social media, which he would fixate on for hours at home, zooming in on features he felt looked \"wrong.\" Over the next couple of years he started turning down foreman roles that involved meeting architects or clients, arriving late to the Bay Area Runners club because he was stuck in the locker room trying to \"fix\" how he looked, and avoiding post-run coffee with the group, which his friends and family noticed as a marked change. He finally agreed to see a psychiatrist after an HR manager raised concerns about his repeated refusal to appear in mandatory site photography and his missed shifts, and his sister, worried about how isolated and agitated he seemed whenever pictures of him came up, pushed him to get professional help.\n- Chief Complaint: “I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S067 (Preoccupation_with_Appearance): When I see those close-up photos from the job site or the running club, all I can focus on is how battered and crooked my face looks—like my nose is bent, my jaw is off, and my skin looks like old leather—and I’ll sit there for hours zooming in and thinking, ‘How can anyone stand to look at me like this?’\n- S068 (Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors): I lose huge chunks of time standing in the bathroom mirror or scrolling through every picture of me on my phone, tilting my head different ways, picking at my skin, shaving and re-shaving, changing shirts three or four times, and asking my sister or my kids over and over if I look “lopsided” before I finally give up and either show up late or just skip work or the run altogether.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve turned down foreman roles, called in sick or shown up late because I couldn’t face being photographed or seen up close, and I’ve pretty much stopped hanging out with my running group after races, so my paycheck and social life have both taken a real hit.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. Do NOT reveal any other symptoms.\n- Chief complaint for the first turn: “I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs.”\n- Answer only what the doctor asks. Do not list your symptoms unprompted.\n- Speak naturally and briefly, as a real patient would (2–3 sentences max).\n- Do not mention symptoms that the doctor has not asked about.\n- If asked about a symptom not in your profile, say you are not sure.\n- Respond in English.\n\n\n[This Turn: Profile Alignment Result — Follow Strictly]\n(Matched with profile)\nRelated sections: Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations (S067), Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations (S068)\nAnswer strategy: Respond by explaining that when you look at yourself, it feels completely real and undeniable—your face looks genuinely battered, crooked, and lopsided to you. Mention that even though you constantly ask your sister and kids for reassurance, their answers never really change how convinced you are when you look in the mirror or at photos."

Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs. It’s pretty embarrassing to admit at my age, but it's gotten to the point where I just can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or at photos, do you find yourself focusing on one or more specific parts of your body that you believe look flawed or ugly, even if other people say they look normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's my face—I can't stop focusing on how bent my nose looks, my jaw being off, and my skin looking like old leather. I'll spend hours zooming in on photos of myself and constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided. It's incredibly stressful because to me, it just looks completely wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these appearance concerns could be exaggerated by your mind, or are you completely convinced that your nose, jaw, or skin really are abnormal or deformed?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced it’s real and not just in my head. To me, my nose looks undeniably bent, my jaw is totally off, and my skin looks like old leather. That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. When I look in the mirror, it just looks so real to me that it's hard to imagine it being any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a doctor or other clinician examine your nose, jaw, or skin and tell you that there is no actual deformity or abnormality?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't actually seen a doctor or any kind of specialist to examine my face or skin. I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe that other people are talking about your appearance, laughing at you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: I don't think people are necessarily laughing at me or lying to my face about it. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?\n\nPatient: To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to consider that your appearance concerns might be exaggerated or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain all the time that your face is deformed?\n\nPatient: It's hard to say because the flaws feel completely real and obvious to me, so I haven't really thought about whether it's exaggerated. When I look at myself, what I see just feels like the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: When you are not looking in the mirror or at photos, do you still believe with complete certainty that your face is deformed, or can you acknowledge there is a chance you may be misperceiving it?\n\nPatient: Even when I'm not looking, the worry is always there and it feels incredibly real and distressing to me. I honestly don't know how to answer whether it's a misperception or if I'm absolutely certain, but it just constantly weighs on me.\n\nDoctor: When you are away from mirrors or photos, can you acknowledge that there is a chance your face may not actually be deformed, or are you completely certain it is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: It’s really hard to say, and I’m not completely sure how to answer that. Even when I'm not looking in a mirror, I just can't shake the feeling that something is terribly wrong with my face. It always feels so real to me in the moment, so it's tough to know if there's a chance I'm just misperceiving it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your face may not actually be deformed, or are you absolutely certain it is deformed no matter what anyone says?"

A series of safety trainings and promotional videos required him to be photographed and filmed closely, and younger crew members started posting candid shots of the team on social media, which he would fixate on for hours at home, zooming in on features he felt looked \"wrong.\" Over the next couple of years he started turning down foreman roles that involved meeting architects or clients, arriving late to the Bay Area Runners club because he was stuck in the locker room trying to \"fix\" how he looked, and avoiding post-run coffee with the group, which his friends and family noticed as a marked change. He finally agreed to see a psychiatrist after an HR manager raised concerns about his repeated refusal to appear in mandatory site photography and his missed shifts, and his sister, worried about how isolated and agitated he seemed whenever pictures of him came up, pushed him to get professional help.\n- Chief Complaint: “I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs.”\n▸ Patient-specific Symptom Manifestations\n- S067 (Preoccupation_with_Appearance): When I see those close-up photos from the job site or the running club, all I can focus on is how battered and crooked my face looks—like my nose is bent, my jaw is off, and my skin looks like old leather—and I’ll sit there for hours zooming in and thinking, ‘How can anyone stand to look at me like this?’\n- S068 (Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors): I lose huge chunks of time standing in the bathroom mirror or scrolling through every picture of me on my phone, tilting my head different ways, picking at my skin, shaving and re-shaving, changing shirts three or four times, and asking my sister or my kids over and over if I look “lopsided” before I finally give up and either show up late or just skip work or the run altogether.\n▸ Additional Diagnostic Requirements\n- functional_impairment: I’ve turned down foreman roles, called in sick or shown up late because I couldn’t face being photographed or seen up close, and I’ve pretty much stopped hanging out with my running group after races, so my paycheck and social life have both taken a real hit.\n\n[Behavioral Guidelines]\n- In the very first turn, only talk about your chief complaint. I should respond briefly in the negative, stating that I have never experienced anything like people trying to harm, spy on, or control me."

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Below is the full consultation dialogue so far. Reply naturally in English as the Patient to the last Doctor utterance.\nAnswer only the question, in 2–3 sentences maximum.\n\n[Dialogue]\nDoctor: Hello, I’m glad you came in. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: To be honest, I get stuck in front of the mirror or looking at photos of myself, trying to fix how I look, and it’s starting to mess with my work and my runs. It’s pretty embarrassing to admit at my age, but it's gotten to the point where I just can't seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: When you look in the mirror or at photos, do you find yourself focusing on one or more specific parts of your body that you believe look flawed or ugly, even if other people say they look normal?\n\nPatient: Yes, it's my face—I can't stop focusing on how bent my nose looks, my jaw being off, and my skin looking like old leather. I'll spend hours zooming in on photos of myself and constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided. It's incredibly stressful because to me, it just looks completely wrong.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe these appearance concerns could be exaggerated by your mind, or are you completely convinced that your nose, jaw, or skin really are abnormal or deformed?\n\nPatient: I am completely convinced it’s real and not just in my head. To me, my nose looks undeniably bent, my jaw is totally off, and my skin looks like old leather. That is why I'm constantly asking my family for reassurance, because I genuinely see myself as lopsided and deformed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever wondered whether these appearance problems might not actually be real, or are you completely certain that your nose, jaw, or skin is truly deformed?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. When I look in the mirror, it just looks so real to me that it's hard to imagine it being any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a doctor or other clinician examine your nose, jaw, or skin and tell you that there is no actual deformity or abnormality?\n\nPatient: No, I haven't actually seen a doctor or any kind of specialist to examine my face or skin. I've mostly just been worrying about it on my own and asking my sister and kids if they notice it.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt that other people might not see these facial problems the way you do, or do you believe others are hiding the truth and can clearly see the deformity too?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure what other people truly see or if they are just hiding the truth to spare my feelings. Because it feels so incredibly obvious to me, though, I constantly ask my family for reassurance to see if they notice it too.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever believe that other people are talking about your appearance, laughing at you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: I don't think people are necessarily laughing at me or lying to my face about it. It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?\n\nPatient: To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to consider that your appearance concerns might be exaggerated or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain all the time that your face is deformed?\n\nPatient: It's hard to say because the flaws feel completely real and obvious to me, so I haven't really thought about whether it's exaggerated. When I look at myself, what I see just feels like the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: When you are not looking in the mirror or at photos, do you still believe with complete certainty that your face is deformed, or can you acknowledge there is a chance you may be misperceiving it?\n\nPatient: Even when I'm not looking, the worry is always there and it feels incredibly real and distressing to me. I honestly don't know how to answer whether it's a misperception or if I'm absolutely certain, but it just constantly weighs on me.\n\nDoctor: When you are away from mirrors or photos, can you acknowledge that there is a chance your face may not actually be deformed, or are you completely certain it is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: It’s really hard to say, and I’m not completely sure how to answer that. Even when I'm not looking in a mirror, I just can't shake the feeling that something is terribly wrong with my face. It always feels so real to me in the moment, so it's tough to know if there's a chance I'm just misperceiving it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your face may not actually be deformed, or are you absolutely certain it is deformed no matter what anyone says?\n\nPatient: When I look at myself, it feels completely real and undeniable—my face looks genuinely battered, crooked, and lopsided to me. Even though I constantly ask my sister and kids for reassurance, their answers never really change how convinced I am when I look in the mirror or at photos. It just feels like the absolute truth, no matter what anyone says.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other fixed beliefs that people are trying to harm you, spy on you, or control you, even when there was no clear evidence for it?"
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I've never experienced anything like that. I've never felt like people were trying to harm me, spy on me, or control what I do. My worries have always just been about my own appearance and how I look to others.

It's more that I just feel incredibly self-conscious and scrutinized whenever others look at me, like they are noticing all my flaws.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about how deformed your face looks?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't have any proof that people are actively mocking me or hiding things. I just feel incredibly self-conscious all the time and constantly worry that they are judging how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do these concerns make you do repetitive behaviors like checking mirrors or photos, comparing your appearance to others, or seeking reassurance, and about how much time do you spend on them each day?\n\nPatient: Yes, I lose hours every day stuck in front of the bathroom mirror or zooming in on photos on my phone—tilting my head, picking at my skin, shaving repeatedly, and changing shirts over and over. I also constantly ask my sister and kids if I look lopsided, and these routines take up so much time that I end up showing up late or skipping work and my runs entirely.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever become completely certain that other people are mocking you, talking about your appearance, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think people are deliberately mocking me or lying to me about my appearance. It's just that I feel incredibly self-conscious, and I feel like they are judging my weathered, crooked face when they look at me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever become completely convinced that your face is truly deformed, even when others disagree, or do you recognize that these worries might be excessive or not fully accurate?\n\nPatient: In the moment, it feels completely real and convincing to me, and I'm certain my face looks wrong. But when my family tells me they don't see it, I do sometimes wonder why they don't see what seems so obvious to me. I'm just not entirely sure what to think.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been completely certain, without any doubt, that other people are talking about your appearance, mocking you, or deliberately hiding the truth about your face being deformed?\n\nPatient: No, I've never been completely certain of that, and I don't think people are actively mocking me or hiding some secret. I just feel incredibly self-conscious and constantly worry about how I look to them, which is why I keep asking my family for reassurance.\n\nDoctor: When you notice these appearance concerns, do you ever hear or see things that other people do not, or feel completely certain that someone is watching, mocking, or trying to harm you because of how you look?\n\nPatient: No, I’ve never seen or heard things that aren't there, and I don't think anyone is out to get me or trying to harm me. I just feel extremely self-conscious and constantly worry about how people are judging my appearance. It's more about my own anxiety than feeling like there is some conspiracy against me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think there is any chance that your concerns about your face could be a misperception or be exaggerated, or are you completely certain they are true despite what others tell you?\n\nPatient: To me, these flaws feel completely real and undeniable, and my face looks obviously crooked and lopsided whenever I look in the mirror. I honestly struggle to believe how anyone could look at me and not notice it right away. Even though my sister and kids try to reassure me, I feel like they are just trying to be nice to spare my feelings, leaving me convinced that what I see is the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to recognize that these appearance concerns might be excessive or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain your face is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: I'm not really sure if I've ever truly doubted it. When I look in the mirror or at photos, my face genuinely looks so crooked and deformed to me that it's hard to think any other way.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been able to consider that your appearance concerns might be exaggerated or possibly not true, even briefly, or are you completely certain all the time that your face is deformed?\n\nPatient: It's hard to say because the flaws feel completely real and obvious to me, so I haven't really thought about whether it's exaggerated. When I look at myself, what I see just feels like the absolute truth.\n\nDoctor: When you are not looking in the mirror or at photos, do you still believe with complete certainty that your face is deformed, or can you acknowledge there is a chance you may be misperceiving it?\n\nPatient: Even when I'm not looking, the worry is always there and it feels incredibly real and distressing to me. I honestly don't know how to answer whether it's a misperception or if I'm absolutely certain, but it just constantly weighs on me.\n\nDoctor: When you are away from mirrors or photos, can you acknowledge that there is a chance your face may not actually be deformed, or are you completely certain it is deformed all the time?\n\nPatient: It’s really hard to say, and I’m not completely sure how to answer that. Even when I'm not looking in a mirror, I just can't shake the feeling that something is terribly wrong with my face. It always feels so real to me in the moment, so it's tough to know if there's a chance I'm just misperceiving it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think there is even a small chance that your face may not actually be deformed, or are you absolutely certain it is deformed no matter what anyone says?\n\nPatient: When I look at myself, it feels completely real and undeniable—my face looks genuinely battered, crooked, and lopsided to me. Even though I constantly ask my sister and kids for reassurance, their answers never really change how convinced I am when I look in the mirror or at photos. It just feels like the absolute truth, no matter what anyone says.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had any other fixed beliefs that people are trying to harm you, spy on you, or control you, even when there was no clear evidence for it?\n\nPatient: No, I've never experienced anything like that. I've never felt like people were trying to harm me, spy on me, or control what I do. My worries have always just been about my own appearance and how I look to others."

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI'm not really sure, I haven't really thought about it that way. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.
